I ran 40 miles in the Saucony Endorphin Speed 6 — this smooth speedster handles every run

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Unlock and manage exclusive Tom’s Guide member rewards.

Join the Tom's Guide Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Saucony Endorphin Speed 6 maintains the fast, versatile ride the line is known for while adding a little more comfort, making it a successful if minor update on the Endorphin Speed 5. It’s one of the best fast trainers available and if you’ve loved past models, the Speed 6 is unlikely to disappoint, but there are some fantastic alternatives to consider too.

Why you can trust Tom's Guide Our writers and editors spend hours analyzing and reviewing products, services, and apps to help find what's best for you. Find out more about how we test, analyze, and rate.

The Saucony Endorphin Speed has been one of the most influential running shoes of recent years, setting the template for fast training sneakers that uses some of the materials and tech from top carbon plate racers.

With the line proving so successful, Saucony has been reluctant to change much about the shoe over the five generations to date, and even though the Endorphin Speed 6 is the biggest update yet thanks to a new midsole foam, it mostly retains the same ride feel as past models.

I’ve reviewed every model of the Endorphin Speed and I’ve always been a big fan. While that’s still true with the Speed 6, this category contains some of the best running shoes available, and there are some lighter, bouncier fast trainers I rate very highly, like the New Balance SC Rebel v1 and Dynafish Xiaonian.

The Saucony Endorphin Speed 6 costs $180 in the U.S. and £180 in the U.K., which is a $5 price rise on the Endorphin Speed 5. It’s an expensive shoe, though not out of line with other plated trainers like the Hoka Mach X3 and New Balance SC Rebel v1.

Right now the Saucony Endorphin Speed 5 is widely reduced, dropping to $104 on Amazon in the Black Friday sales, which is a factor to consider when weighing up buying the Speed

The Endorphin Speed 6 is currently available in five colors in the women’s shoe, and four colors in the men’s, including the white and yellow design I tested.

There is a wide version of the shoe available, though I found that the standard one fit me well in my normal running shoe size, the same size I’ve used with all past models of the shoe.

Get instant access to breaking news, the hottest reviews, great deals and helpful tips.

Saucony has added 2mm of foam to the midsole of the Endorphin Speed 6, which has a stack height of 38mm at the heel and 30mm at the forefoot, maintaining the 8mm drop of past models.

It weighs in at 8.5oz, which is 0.5oz lighter than the Endorphin Speed 5 in my size, a welcome drop for a speed-focused shoe.

The Endorphin Speed 6 has a simple mesh upper that has a little bit of padding around the tongue and collar, but mostly focuses on being lightweight and breathable.

It’s my preferred kind of upper on any shoe, since it’s not oppressively padded on the tongue or overly stiff around the heel, and I found the fit comfortable and secure throughout all my runs.

The Endorphin Speed 6’s main changes are in the midsole, which is made from a new foam called PWRRUN PBX, which is meant to be lighter and bouncier than the PWRRUN PB that has been used on all past models of the shoe.

There’s also the higher stack and a redesigned nylon plate in the midsole, which aims to create a smoother, faster ride, with the Speedroll rocker on the shoe also contributing to this.

The changes to the midsole are noticeable on the run, though not as dramatically as I expected when looking at the spec sheet for the Speed 6.

Rubber is used sparingly on the bottom of the Speed 6 to keep the weight down, but patches cover the forefoot and heel of the shoe, and in my experience Saucony’s pelletized midsole foams are durable even when the foam is exposed on the bottom of a shoe.

Grip hasn’t always been a positive feature on the Speed line, but the Speed 6 provided me with reliable traction in wet conditions on both the road and the track during my testing.

After the very pleasant surprise of the original Endorphin Speed, which I ran over 300 miles in, I’ve found that the line has delivered a reliably similar experience with all of the models since.

There have been updates to make it more stable and comfortable, but ultimately all five versions of the Speed to date have had a smooth, fast and versatile ride feel.

This is still true of the Endorphin Speed 6 for me, and while that’s mostly a positive in that it performed well across a variety of runs in my testing, some might have hoped for a bigger change to challenge rivals like the SC Rebel v1 and cheaper, lighter options like the Adidas Adizero Evo SL and Dynafish Xiaonian.

I used the shoe for a couple of easy runs, two progression runs moving from easy to tempo pace, and a threshold track workout running 4 x 8min reps, and it felt great throughout.

I used the shoe for a couple of easy runs, two progression runs moving from easy to tempo pace, and a threshold track workout running 4 x 8min reps, and it felt great throughout.

The rockered midsole pushes you smoothly through your footstrike, and while the midsole foam isn’t as soft and springy as some, it does give a good amount of energy return off the toes.

I also found the Speed 6 to be quite stable and comfortable for easy runs, which is not always the case with plated trainers like this. I could happily use it for pretty much any kind of run, though it’s not as fast as a true carbon plate racer, or as comfortable as a max-cushioned cruiser.

To compare it directly to the Endorphin Speed 5 I did a short run wearing both shoes, and while the Speed 6 is a little bouncier and more comfortable, especially under the forefoot, I still felt the two shoes had a similar ride feel.

The Speed 6 is still a bit firmer than most super-trainers, and not as comfortable as more cushioned shoes under the toes. Some will like this more direct ride feel, but I appreciate the extra softness and bounce of a shoe like the SC Rebel v1.

If you’re a long term fan of the Endorphin Speed line the Speed 6 will be a winner. It’s a mild improvement on the previous models with extra cushion and bounce, and delivers the versatile performance you expect from the line.

There is certainly a case for getting the Speed 5 instead in sales in my opinion, but the Speed 6 is a slightly better shoe overall and a successful update.

The Speed line has been faced with increasingly fierce competition in recent years, and I don’t think the Speed 6 does much to change the equation on this front.

If you want a lighter, bouncier fast trainer, the New Balance SC Rebel v1 would be my pick, while plate-free shoes like the Adidas Evo SL, Dyanfish Xiaonian and the new 361° Mega 3 Pro all offer fast, fun rides at a lower price.

As someone who would always have had the Endorphin Speed in my own rotation through the first few models, I’ve now moved on to shoes like this myself. Even though the Speed 6 is just as good as past models, I personally like the springier ride available in fast trainers.

Nick Harris-Fry is an experienced health and fitness journalist, writing professionally since 2012. He spent nine years working on the Coach magazine and website before moving to the fitness team at Tom’s Guide in 2024. Nick is a keen runner and also the founder of YouTube channel The Run Testers, which specialises in reviewing running shoes, watches, headphones and other gear.

Nick ran his first marathon in 2016 and became obsessed with the sport. He now has PBs of 2hr 25min for the marathon and 15min 30sec for 5K. Nick is also a qualified Run Leader in the UK.

Nick is an established expert in the fitness area and along with writing for many publications, including Live Science, Expert Reviews, Wareable, Coach and Get Sweat Go, he has been quoted on The Guardian and The Independent.

The Premier League’s newest managers have a lot to lose

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Farewell then, Michael Carrick and Pierre Sage, who have gone off to the great big Premier League managerial scrapheap in the sky after an international break where only the identity of the next James Bond could rival speculation over Carrick’s eventual replacement in particular.

In Mauricio Pochettino and Thomas Frank respectively, Manchester United and Crystal Palace have gone for a pair of managers whose reputations in English football could do with a good start this weekend to avoid the varying levels of doom that have surrounded Old Trafford and Selhurst Park from peaking again.

It’s a good thing, then, that despite their contrasting styles, both are known for putting emphasis on energy – not just in how their team plays on the pitch, but in how the culture around the club feels off it.

Pochettino has made headlines before for his love of lemons (they absorb negative energy, he’ll have you know) and his apparent sixth sense. In his 2017 book Brave New World, he wrote: ‘What most influences my decisions is my ability to see if the right energy is flowing.’

What kind of energy he felt flowing through director of football Jason Wilcox is anyone’s guess at this stage, but his emphasis on things like togetherness and the mindset of his players could finally be the key to starting to turn the Suez canal tanker that United have become, on the pitch at least.

Read exclusive analysis of the biggest stories in football with Metro's weekly football newsletter In The Mixer. Sign up now for free.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

If Pochettino comes across as part high-level manager, part yoga teacher, then Frank has the air of an executive ready to rebuild the culture at an ailing business.

A year off from football was on the cards after he was sacked by Tottenham in February. The Dane turned down Palace during the summer in favour of World Cup punditry and attending the Tour de France, and didn’t sound completely convinced he’d made the right decision to cut that break short.

‘I was a little bit, “When do I want to go back in? Is it now?” Then this opportunity came up,’ he told PalaceTV this week. ‘I was thinking, this is probably too good to turn down. When I’m sitting here, walking around the training ground, it was just, “Okay, you’re back”, which scared me a little bit, but it’s good.’ Consider the troops rallied.

Questions surround the suitability of both appointments, as Pochettino’s biggest success at Spurs was built on the back of a young, mobile team with something to prove and Frank’s at Brentford came as part of a well-oiled machine. You’d be waiting a long time for anyone to describe United or Palace as either of those these days.

Use AI to go deeper into the stories you care about – powered by Metro and trusted publications.

There’s hope to be found too, for those willing to look for it. Frank did only really have half a bad season and the perceived wisdom is that his personality will suit a ‘smaller’ club – as long as he stays away from any Brighton-branded coffee cups – while of all the managers tasked with fixing United, Pochettino does have the air of a man who can become a messianic figure.

Managerial reputations can only survive a certain number of kicks before there’s no coming back and there may not be many chances left for either man at this level if things go pear-shaped.

However, with both teams playing pretty much every three days for the next month, there are plenty of opportunities for their new managers to shift the vibes back towards a positive setting.

And even if the wins don’t start flowing, transfer enthusiasts should have something to cling on to as Palace and United are reportedly ready to back their new men with some signings once the January window opens.

It’s nice to have something to look forward to after Christmas, at least.

Worse Than 2008? Peter Schiff Issues Terrifying Market Crash Warning

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Noted economist and longtime Bitcoin critic Peter Schiff has sounded the alarm about an upcoming financial apocalypse, warning that the crisis could be worse than the historic 2008 market crash.

On Nov. 20, Peter Schiff shared a series of troubling X posts warning about the potential for significant losses in the U.S. stock market, real estate, and cryptocurrencies amid escalating financial system stress.

The economist shared a video with a very alarming headline: "The Next Crash Will Be Worse Than 2008."

In one of his most recent posts on X, Schiff argued that the recent gold price downtrend might signal a grim outlook for other key asset classes.

"It's probably a bad sign that gold is falling this much. It probably means that the stock and real estate markets are going to fall even more in percentage terms," Schiff cautioned.

According to Peter Schiff, gold's recent price behavior could be signaling deflation. As a result, this might lead to a more severe financial crisis.

However, Peter Schiff is convinced that the Federal Reserve will step in to prevent this financial apocalypse.

"Gold is signaling deflation. But asset deflation would bust the government and the banking system, so the Fed will pivot to avoid it," the economist explained.

Simply put, Schiff assumes that the Federal Reserve will pivot and introduce monetary stimulus to prevent a financial meltdown.

In another startling prediction, the economist warned that the U.S. stock market might wipe out all its gains since President Donald Trump took office.

"There's a good chance that the U.S. stock market is about to give back all the gains since Trump took office," the economist predicted.

He also warned that there might be a recession. As a result, Trump will likely point to Democrats and their wins in the recent midterm elections as the reason for this recession.

Interestingly enough, Peter Schiff's bearish outlook also applies to Bitcoin (BTC), which has already suffered significant losses amid the overall market crash.

On Nov. 18, Peter Schiff shared several alarming market indicators. According to his data, the Nasdaq has dropped 12.25%. AI infrastructure company CoreWeave has plummeted 64%.

Bitcoin, on the other hand, has plunged 33.5% from its 2026 peak.

Additionally, the economist noted that the U.S. housing market index had dropped to 25, its lowest level since 2012. This shows that the economic issues go well beyond AI stocks.

"Still think this is just about AI?" he asked.

Gold Price Prediction 2027: Goldman Sachs Forecasts Gold at $4,650

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Log in to access your notifications and stay updated. If you’re not a member yet, Sign Up to get started!

Nidhi is a Certified Digital Marketing Executive and Passionate crypto Journalist covering the world of alternative currencies. She shares the latest and trending news on Cryptocurrency and Blockchain.

Nidhi is a Certified Digital Marketing Executive and Passionate crypto Journalist covering the world of alternative currencies. She shares the latest and trending news on Cryptocurrency and Blockchain.

Nidhi is a Certified Digital Marketing Executive and Passionate crypto Journalist covering the world of alternative currencies. She shares the latest and trending news on Cryptocurrency and Blockchain.

Gold prices could reach $4,650 by the end of 2027, according to Goldman Sachs. The bank expects rising interest rates to create short-term pressure on gold, while continued central bank purchases could support prices over the longer term.

Goldman Sachs expects the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates again in December, followed by another hike in March 2027. Higher rates could strengthen the US dollar and make bonds and savings accounts more attractive than gold, which does not generate interest income.

Central banks are buying approximately 60 tonnes of gold per month, compared with an average of 17 tonnes before 2022.

Purchases increased after Russia’s foreign exchange reserves were frozen in 2022, prompting central banks to reconsider their dependence on foreign-held financial assets.

France and the Netherlands have also been moving gold reserves from foreign vaults to domestic storage. These developments indicate continued focus on gold as a reserve asset.

This sustained demand could support gold prices even if higher interest rates cause short-term declines.

Higher interest rates generally put pressure on gold by strengthening the US dollar and increasing the returns available from bonds and cash.

Goldman Sachs also expects institutional demand for gold-related options to decline towards historical averages as interest rates rise.

The 2022 market cycle offers a comparison. Gold initially fell approximately 20% during the Federal Reserve’s aggressive rate hikes, while central banks continued accumulating the metal. Gold subsequently recovered over the following three years.

Goldman Sachs expects gold to outperform silver in 2027.

Central banks purchase gold for their reserves but do not have similar demand for silver. Silver prices depend more heavily on investment demand and industrial consumption.

Higher interest rates could slow economic activity and weaken industrial demand, making silver more volatile than gold.

Goldman Sachs forecasts gold at $5,600 by the end of 2028. The bank expects the Federal Reserve to begin cutting interest rates in November 2027. Lower rates could reduce the opportunity cost of holding gold and support investment demand.

For 2027, Federal Reserve policy and central bank purchases remain the key factors in Goldman’s gold price outlook.

UK and EU to agree youth mobility scheme, BBC understands

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Watch: Burnham says he wants 'best deal for Britain' at UK-EU summit

Up to 100,000 young people from the EU would be allowed to travel, work and study in the UK per year under a new youth mobility scheme set to be agreed on Friday, the BBC understands.

The same number of young Britons would be allowed into Europe under the scheme, expected to be approved at the UK-EU summit in Brussels. Downing Street has not commented on the details, but said talks were "down to the wire" on Thursday.

The prime minister has said a "Channel action plan" on immigration could also be struck, while negotiators are seeking agreements on food trade rules and carbon emissions pricing.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch has accused Andy Burnham of "reopening the divisions" of the Brexit referendum.

A new youth mobility scheme would aim to make it easier for young people to work and travel both in the EU and UK.

The UK already has schemes in place with 13 non-EU countries including Australia, Canada and India.

In 2024, the EU proposed allowing 18 to 30 year-olds to work or study in the EU and UK for up to four years.

Labour, which was then in opposition, rejected the deal - arguing it amounted to reinstating freedom of movement.

Since coming to government, the party has suggested it would consider a more restricted scheme with a time limit, a cap on the numbers coming to the UK and a ban on participants from claiming benefits.

The UK's request to limit numbers has proved to be a sticking point in negotiation ahead of the summit, along with the EU's request that EU students should pay the same tuition fees at UK universities as domestic students.

Speaking to broadcasters on Thursday evening, Burnham was asked if there were any circumstances in which the UK would walk away from a deal on Friday.

"There would always be those circumstances because I'm in this to get the best deal for Britain, always," he said.

"But what's in it for us is the possibility of lower food prices and I can't turn my back on that, lower costs for business, cutting red tape, and a really big win potentially of more collaboration on immigration - a Channel action plan - which is a really big deal if we can land that tomorrow.

"So I hope people can understand why I have pushed this because it could be a really good deal for Britain."

Burnham previously said the UK and EU could link up their law enforcement efforts, including sharing more data and criminal records of foreign nationals.

The prime minister's spokesman has said if a deal were secured, the government would introduce legislation to Parliament next week to implement changes.

The Brexit debate is back â and there are nerves about what happens next

Faisal Islam: What's in the Brexit reset food deal and are there trade-offs?

The EU that Burnham is eyeing up is very different to the one Britain left

An agreement on trade could remove checks and paperwork faced by food companies exporting to the EU.

The government argues this could add Â£5bn to the economy and cut food prices.

However, it could mean the UK will have to follow certain EU rules on products, for example on gene-edited crops or labelling.

Burnham has also said the summit would allow the UK to raise concerns about the Made in Europe scheme, which is designed to protect industries from unfair Chinese competition.

Burnham has previously suggested he could commit Labour to building a closer long-term future relationship with Europe, including asking to rejoin the organisation as a full member - however, he has ruled out major changes before the next election.

The party's 2024 manifesto promised not to return to the EU, the single market, customs union or freedom of movement, which allows EU citizens to live and work elsewhere in the bloc without a visa.

Earlier this week, former deputy prime minister David Lammy urged the prime minister to go into the next general election promising to rejoin the EU.

However, the subject has divided his cabinet with one minister warning of the costs that would come with reversing Brexit.

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage, speaking to The Sun, external, said he does "not believe for a minute" the country would "ever vote to rejoin" the EU.

He added: "I think what Burnham is doing is playing a very cynical political game. He's trying to pick up votes from the Lib Dems, pick up votes from the Greens, and using it as a massive distraction from all the problems that he can't deal with."

Speaking to the BBC's Today programme on Thursday, Badenoch said she wanted to see easier trading with the EU, but "not trade at any cost".

She also said the new deals could see UK companies having to follow EU rules, even those that do not trade with the bloc.

Appearing on BBC Breakfast on Friday, Reform UK leader Nigel Farage described the agreement as a "very, very expensive and costly error" and said Burnham has no "democratic mandate to do any of this".

He added that the Labour party manifesto was clear that the UK would not go back towards the single market and asked: "Where is our democracy?".

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey told BBC Newsnight that while he agreed with Burnham on "the harms of Brexit", he did not think the prime minister was "going to go anywhere near far enough".

"We need to go further and faster," he said, including returning to freedom of movement - which Sir Ed said would help tackle immigration and boost the UK economy.

"I can tell you wrote this yourself"

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Home Blog What I'm Doing Now Photo Guestbook

Those were some of the first words my honours project supervisor said to me last week when we met to discuss a progress report I had written to mark the midway point of the project.

It's a roughly 10-page report on the control of flapping-wing micro aerial vehicles - which is probably very boring unless you're particularly interested in that. I accidentally set the due date for the report a week early in my calendar, so after spending 48 hours stressed and pumping out 5,000 words, citing references and adding diagrams1, I was pleasantly relieved to find I'd tricked myself into being quite productive. I had a lot of time to get my supervisor to review the report and action his feedback.

When he told me he could tell I wrote it myself, I wasn't sure how to take it. A compliment? An insult? Then he followed it up with:

"I've been sent a lot of reports written by AI."

At first this shocked me. Our reports are limited to 10 pages - so the appendices are getting a big workout around here - how hard is it to right 10 pages yourself?

But then I thought about what the last year of my engineering degree has been like.

Assignment documents that start with "you are strongly encouraged to explore the use of AI" which quickly turns into the clear understanding that you aren't just encouraged but expected to use it. That you're disadvantaged by not using it. The hardest class of my degree was spent in a brick-walled windowless computer lab prompting an AI for eight hours a day.

The reason why we've been pushed to use it?

Apparently it's the way the industry is going. Apparently actually knowing things doesn't matter nearly so much as knowing how to prompt a machine to do the thing. Apparently the future of engineering - at least in my field - is to become a glorified babysitter. The AI isn't going anywhere, they say, so it's best we learn how to manage it well.

But sitting in a room telling an AI "ignore the approaching deadline, we still have 8 hours, keep working" isn't exactly filling me with enthusiasm for the rest of my career.

It's soul crushing, and I don't want to outsource so much of my thinking to it.

In a world where people are using AI to summarise books instead of reading them and turning to AI for therapy, I don't want to keep using it. It feels like such a slippery slope. As literacy and numeracy rates decline, I can't help but question whether encouraging us to rely on AI so heavily is a good idea.

It makes me wonder: are we still thinking for ourselves at all?

Because I worry that it starts with using it for an assignment when you're encouraged to, but then it turns into relying on it to write a 10-page report on a project you should know back-to-front. A project that you'll have to present and defend in person when it finishes. And then you're asking it for advice, using it to summarise books, turning to it for anything you would normally look up or ask a friend.

The internet is riddled with AI content that is making it increasingly more difficult to tell fact from fiction. In a world where everything is so polarised and deliberately provoking, the ability to identify bias or motivation in the content we consume has perhaps never been more important. Being able to think for ourselves has perhaps never been more important.

As the tech world continues to offer us increasingly more opportunities to automate things, I'm practically begging you to please not automate your thinking.

Read the books. Write the papers. Search for answers instead of asking AI. Ask a friend. Ask your mum about the mystery illness you're convinced you're developing and then take her advice and try drinking a glass of water.

Think about what the creators of the content you consume want you to think2. Treat the things you consume like the Year 7 English class where you were taught how to identify persuasive techniques. Think about things as much as possible.

Please just don't get the AI do it for you.

On a more positive note, here's some recent good things:

I think what I want you to think here is probably pretty obvious.↩

Mario Frick savours Aberdeen ‘paradise’ as heavy metal football takes precedence over cynical rave murmurings

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Although he rates it as one of his best football memories, Mario Frick learned a painful lesson after scoring against Scotland at Hampden 16 years ago.

He injured himself in the resulting melee with his Liechtenstein teammates and had to be substituted. “They gave me an injection but it didn't help,” he recalled. "In the seventh minute of stoppage time we lost 2-1 - which is football. It was my last year in international football, maybe my last goal for the international team at around 36-years-old.”

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

It was a reminder of the perils of over celebrating. The cynic might say the new Aberdeen manager has come to the right place. The Pittodrie side haven’t scored for three matches and have recorded the fewest shots on target in the league this season.

There doesn’t seem much danger of him erupting with joy and bumping his head on the roof of those tight Pittodrie dugouts. Albeit, he will have to wait for the chance to take a bow at the stadium having replaced the sacked Stephen Robinson during the international window. Trips to Rugby Park and then Ibrox await before he can enjoy the comforts of home for the first time against Dundee at the end of this month.

Although there was no game on at Pittodrie on Thursday, Frick's face adorned the jumbo screen in the corner as he was officially welcomed to the club ahead of being “formally unveiled”, as the club described it. Even the empty stadium, he said, gives him “goosebumps”. He regards it as a temple. Nowhere is the reverential air more intoxicating than in the Sir Alex Ferguson Lounge, where Frick was presented to reporters for the first time. “Pure football,” he said, while gazing at the photographs adorning the walls.

The Aberdeen training facility at Cormack Park is better than anything he encountered in Switzerland, where he played and managed. “It is paradise,” he said. He’s clearly keen to linger awhile, to which the reply might be: Good luck with that. While he occupies a unique position as the first Liechtenstein player to become a professional footballer, Frick is slightly less exceptional when it comes to Aberdeen.

The 52-year-old is the 27th permanent manager at Pittodrie, stretching back to 1903. That averages out at about one every four-and-a-half years, which isn’t too bad. However, as many as 16 have come and gone since Ferguson left for Old Trafford 40 years ago this month. The recent anniversary celebrations – Manchester United being the ones celebrating rather than Aberdeen, who have rued the day almost ever since – were a reminder of the significance of that November’s morning in 1986, when it was announced he was heading south.

No club is immune to the difficulties of trying to replace a successful manager, as Manchester United have spent the last 13 years proving. Aberdeen have not been completely feckless without Fergie. A Scottish Cup here, a League Cup there. They haven’t been relegated either. Not yet at least.

Steering the side away from danger is Frick’s first task, starting this weekend against the only team currently below them: Kilmarnock. He knows what’s involved having taken over at his previous club, Luzern, five years ago when they were deep in relegation trouble. They parted company this summer.

“They were bottom of the table with 18 matches and 11 points when I took over there and the whole environment was similar to now,” he said. “We saved them and progressed from there. I am sure I can do the same now at Aberdeen. I am very ambitious and now it is important to get out of this situation and we want to go to the top six.”

The noises coming out of Pittodrie in recent days is that Frick likes to play ‘front foot, attacking football’, which seems to mirror the promises made by every new manager these days. Isn’t that what Sebastien Pocognoli trumpeted when he was unveiled as Scotland manager three months ago? The alarm bells might be growing louder for Aberdeen fans. Frick explained his “heavy metal football” philosophy. “It is high pressing, it is counter pressing, it is vertical play and creating many goalscoring chances,” he said. “That is heavy metal.”

Supporters will argue that anything’s better than what a team coached by Frick’s predecessor was producing. Aberdeen chief executive Adrian Bevington referred to the new manager's “warm personality” and suggested local journalists should look forward to getting to know the new man. Frick is an urbane character though one it might be advisable to avoid crossing. Trim and sporting a military haircut, he doesn’t look as if he will accept having his authority challenged.

As for the vexed question of the captaincy, with Kevin Nisbet giving the impression that the responsibility weighs heavily on him, Frick confirmed the striker will retain the armband against Kilmarnock. “Afterwards, I will have to decide what we do,” he said.

Frick speaks five languages with various levels of proficiency. His German and Italian are perfect. “English I need to improve,” he said. “I started learning English in school but never used it.” He recently started English tuition online “with an Australian who lives in France” with a view to working at a British club. “My only ambition now is to bring success to Aberdeen,” he said.

The fans will hope that such a zeal for self-improvement is catching. When Ferguson once ordered his players to have no fears about going to Glasgow and winning, now a sense of trepidation infects so much at the club. Aberdeen have just announced that a “Hacienda Classical” rave night event at Pittodrie on 28 May will no longer be going ahead. Someone queried online whether it was a case of the calendar needing to be left clear for relegation play-offs. Confidence is rock bottom.

Frick relishes the challenge. “I am ready!” he said. “I have had a five-month holiday! I am full of energy.”

After the international break, this is a transfer-heavy gameweek with players being bought and sold in bulk.

Whether you're planning on wildcarding or making wholesale changes and taking the hit, this is essential reading for all FPL managers getting back into the fantasy football groove.

Here is my verdict on the most popular names.

Jan Paul van Hecke (£5.1m): One of the best defenders to own. He is guaranteed to play, reliable for defensive contributions and a threat from set-pieces. Spurs' next three fixtures are Ipswich, Sunderland and Fulham, which is incredibly solid. Verdict: BUY

Luka Vuskovic (£5.3m): Brighton have kept the joint-most clean sheets this season, so he looks superb value. Maxim De Cuyper has grabbed the headlines, but Vuskovic is only five points behind and the second-highest scoring defender in the game. Strong defensive contributions, good attacking data and safe minutes explain it. Hull away, then Newcastle, Everton and Ipswich at home makes four of the next six fixtures too good to pass up. Verdict: BUY

Ezri Konsa (£4.9m): Over 160,000 managers have bought him, which surprises me. He is a hold or a sell depending on your team, but never a buy. He is fine for a couple more gameweeks, but feels like a transfer out waiting to happen, as William Saliba is set for a return.

I think the Frenchman is being saved for the Manchester City game in Gameweek 12. Konsa is worth owning if you already have Vuskovic and Van Hecke, but if selling him is your only route to either, make that move. Verdict: HOLD/SELL

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Lewis Hall (£5.2m): A poor defence and a tough run of fixtures. With Van Hecke and Vuskovic available, Hall is an easy sell. Verdict: SELL

Josko Gvardiol (£5.6m): He costs a premium, and Manchester City are not in the best form for Expected Goals Conceded (xGC). He rarely earns defensive contribution points, so he relies solely on attacking threat. In my book, he's not worth it when the alternatives offer more, with zero headaches over minutes heading into the congested period. Verdict: SELL

Marc Guehi (£6.0m): For similar reasons to Gvardiol, he is an easy sell, with no need to second guess starts of a player at this price every week. Verdict: SELL

Bryan Mbeumo (£7.9m): He has come close to returning several times in the last three gameweeks and been unlucky not to. Manchester United could get a new manager bounce, and from Gameweek 12 they face Brentford (H), Newcastle (A), Coventry (H) and Crystal Palace (A), so I would hold. Verdict: HOLD

Kevin Schade (£6.1m): He has been a huge disappointment so far, but is worth holding onto. Igor Thiago is suspended, which should hand Schade penalty duties at home to Everton. He could be a smart differential. Verdict: HOLD

Marcus Tavernier (£6.7m): Over 150,000 managers have bought him this week, which is understandable given Bournemouth’s fixtures. He is on four yellow cards though, and one away from a suspension, so I think he is worth dodging until the suspension risk is out of the way. Verdict: HOLD

Joao Pedro (£7.6m): He is the most sold player of the gameweek at the time of writing. With his return close, I would stay put. Managers who swapped him for Nicolas Jackson or Thierno Barry have gained recently, but if you have kept him this long, Chelsea’s fixtures make it sensible to carry on. I do not own him, and I plan to buy in Gameweek 13, as I expect him to be a good hold for the foreseeable future from that point on. Verdict: HOLD

Dominic Calvert-Lewin (£6.0m): Recent returns have been underwhelming, so a lot of managers are getting rid. Leeds host Coventry in Gameweek 12, though, and with Erling Haaland away at Arsenal he could be a differential captain. I will bench him at Chelsea this week, play him in Gameweek 12, then decide in Gameweek 13. Verdict: HOLD

This 16-Year-Old Founded an AI Missile Startup. He’s Targeting ‘Cost per Kill’

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At 16 years old, Henrijs Dandzbergs is too young to vote. Or drink. Or smoke. Or drive unsupervised. But he’s not too young to win half a million dollars for his AI-guided-missile startup.

In September, Dandzbergs, who was born in Latvia, was admitted to Y Combinator’s fall 2026 cohort. The startup accelerator counts companies like Airbnb, Stripe, DoorDash, and Reddit among its alumni; Dandzbergs is likely the youngest solo founder in its vaunted history.

His startup, Polemos Industries, is attempting to develop a new class of precision-guided missile that can be easily and cheaply manufactured, can be fired from portable launchers, and can resist jamming attempts. “I think missiles are the coolest thing,” Dandzbergs tells WIRED. “A lot of people that have found success in engineering early on have had a passion for blowing stuff up.”

In conversation, Dandzbergs is poised and articulate. He talks about “attacking hard problems,” breaking things down to “first principles,” and how the Ukraine conflict has forever altered the theater of war—a personal fixation, given his country's proximity to Russia. He projects an unassailable confidence in his own abilities.

“He’s a pretty exceptional person,” says Harj Taggar, a YC partner assigned to mentor Dandzbergs. “I’m still somewhat in disbelief that we’re talking about a 16-year-old.”

Dandzbergs has resolved to apply these unusual talents to figuring out how to optimize targeted killing. His startup’s guiding ambition, according to its investor one-pager, is to bring down the “cost per kill.”

As a young(er) kid, Dandzbergs was rarely in one place for long; his family moved between Latvia, Indonesia, Australia, and Spain. His surfer father, part owner of an insurance company, likes to go wherever he might find the raddest waves.

Early in his schooling, which mostly took place online, Dandzbergs demonstrated a clear talent for science and math. His room started to look like a lab, littered with schematics, robotics, Raspberry Pis, and 3D-printed components.

When Covid struck, Dandzbergs’ family returned to Latvia. His parents enrolled him in Astra Nova, an Elon Musk–linked school where students undergo an experimental curriculum built around lateral thinking and problem-solving instead of rote memorization. Dandzbergs took a class taught by a NASA employee and became fascinated by aerospace engineering. His lab creations became ever more elaborate: a bionic arm, a thermal control system for a model rocket, sensors for a self-flying drone.

Around this time, Russia invaded Ukraine. In Latvia, Dandzbergs started to see military jets overhead, and stray missiles were intercepted at the Russian border. With the sovereignty of Eastern European nations under threat, new defense startups began to pop up across the region. Dandzbergs wanted in.

“A patriotic intention to protect my country, European countries, and Ukrainian and American soldiers, and understanding the future of autonomous warfare—that’s my drive,” he says.

To stretch his technical skills, Dandzbergs started to take part in hackathons, where software and hardware engineers compete to develop the most sophisticated prototype from scratch.

In April of this year, he flew to London to take part in a hackathon held by Unicorn Mafia, an invitation-only group of developers. The prize included $10,000 in cash, a plane ticket to San Francisco, and free legal assistance with O-1 visa applications to stay in the US.

Less than 48 hours later, Dandzbergs and his two teammates, Aleksandrs Tihenko and Kai Yang, had built an AI system that could design hardware circuit boards from natural language prompts, much like how a software engineer might use AI to generate code. Though his teammates are both a few years older, Dandzbergs pushed for them to include even more intricate features in the prototype. “It was amazing,” says Tihenko. “The imagination of Henrijs in the hardware space is just crazy.”

Seeing that they stood no chance of beating Dandzbergs’ group and other front-runners, teams started to drop out of the competition, according to Fergus McKenzie-Wilson, Unicorn Mafia’s director and one of the hackathon judges. “What they built was incredibly impressive,” he says. “No one had ever seen that before.”

When the organizers crowned Dandzbergs’ team victorious, somebody handed him the mic. “I’m 16 years old,” he said.

The week after the hackathon, Dandzbergs and his teammates pitched their circuit board concept to YC. They won a place in the summer program, but Dandzbergs wavered over whether he should drop out of high school. The trio also sparred over whether the startup should focus on applying the technology to the defense sector, Dandzbergs’ primary area of interest. Dandzbergs ended up pulling out of the partnership. “It was hard,” says Tihenko. “But ultimately we just settled that the best decision for the company would be to part ways and focus on different things.”

By the summer, Danzbergs was set on building a defense startup and reapplied to YC on his own. He had spotted a gap in the market, he claimed, for AI-guided missiles that were more precise and could carry bigger warheads than the drones deployed widely in Ukraine. His products, he added, would be cheaper and easier to manufacture than hulking great rocket systems.

YC initially tried to steer him away. Dandzbergs was promising a lot of everything, all at once. It wasn’t clear whether his idea could work or whether pulling out of school was the right thing for him to do. “There’s a paternal element to it,” says Taggar. “You don’t want to be screwing up people's lives—and working on a startup is a pretty serious thing.”

Dandzbergs is up against a fleet of defense companies—from Anduril to CoAspire to Leidos—aiming to develop cheaper, mass-producible missiles. The more sophisticated the guidance system, the more difficult that task becomes.

“You can build a cheap missile that doesn’t go very far. You can build a cheap missile that doesn’t carry much of a payload. You can build a cheap missile that has a low reliability rate,” says Tom Karako, director of the Missile Defense Project and senior fellow at the Center for Strategic and International Studies. “There are trade-offs.”

But Dandzbergs wasn’t going to be dissuaded, and YC figured that somebody else would probably invest in his idea. The incubator invited him to join its fall program. “Big picture, you never know if these things are possible or not,” says Taggar. “Is it a non-zero-percent chance that it could be possible? That’s frankly the threshold, especially if it’s a really big [idea].”

So far, according to Dandzbergs, Polemos Industries has developed the software system that will guide its missiles and has begun to hammer out elements of the hardware design. By the time the YC program finishes in December, he hopes to be able to showcase a prototype missile.

If he does ultimately succeed, though, Dandzbergs will need to confront an assortment of hairy ethical quandaries. Who should have access to these new missiles? What if a technical error were to result in friendly fire? Can he make peace with his technology being used to end somebody’s life?

Dandzbergs claims that he has come to terms with these issues. He says he takes solace in the belief that his motivations are pure: to protect the US and its European allies.

By his logic, autonomous weapons are an inevitable part of the future, so he might as well be the one who builds them. “I choose not to shy away from the emergence of modern conflict as a global issue. I simply don’t have a lot of respect for people that do,” he says. “I want to be part of it. I want to move with the future of warfare.”

But others who have found themselves in a similar position say it’s difficult to know in advance how somebody will deal with the knowledge that their invention has killed people.

McKenzie-Wilson, the Unicorn Mafia director, launched a drone startup in his early twenties. Initially, he had meant for the drones to evacuate wounded soldiers, but after a missile company invested, his startup retooled its technology to drop payloads too. He left around the same time.

“I don’t think I was emotionally mature enough,” he says. “[Dandzbergs] will realize at some point. He’ll either have an issue with it or he won’t.”

Polemos Industries announced itself publicly on November 5 with a glossy social media video. Set to the ticking of a clock, it featured a set of four missiles fired into the sky from ground launchers, then blowing a convoy of four military vehicles to smithereens.

The video climbed to the top of the trending page on X. In the responses, a couple of people tagged Palmer Luckey, cofounder of Anduril, who was 19 when he started his first company. “Coming for him,” Dandzbergs responded.

Dandzbergs recognizes that people are likely to dismiss him for his age. But he considers youth one of his biggest advantages. “Please don’t say I’m sacrificing my childhood,” he says. “This is the childhood I dreamed of.”

Mark Noble Reveals Important Change Behind West Ham’s Transfer Plans

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Mark Noble has provided an interesting insight into how West Ham United’s recruitment operation is working under Scott Parker, revealing that Nils Koppen is in regular contact with the coaching staff about the players the club needs.

Speaking in an interview with West Ham’s official website, Noble explained that the Director of Player Recruitment is arriving at Rush Green as early as 6.30am, watching training and discussing the squad with Parker and his coaches.

With the January transfer window approaching and West Ham desperate to secure promotion, it’s encouraging to hear that recruitment discussions are taking place. But for me, the most important part of Noble’s revelation has very little to do with what time Koppen arrives for work.

Quite frankly, whether Koppen arrives at half six in the morning is neither here nor there.

Much of the analytical and recruitment work can be carried out perfectly well from wherever somebody can plug into the internet.

What I find far more important is the open dialogue Noble has described.

He explained that Koppen watches training and that the coaching staff speak openly with him about the players they believe West Ham need.

Koppen then works with the club’s scouts and analysts in the background, allowing Parker and Noble to concentrate on coaching.

That strikes me as a huge plus and potentially a massive step in the right direction.

It also appears to represent a departure from the way things have operated previously, when questions were regularly raised about whether recruitment decisions matched the manager’s requirements.

As we discussed following Parker’s talkSPORT interview, the manager is absolutely right to concentrate on improving the players already at his disposal rather than becoming consumed by January transfers.

But that only works if the recruitment department understands precisely what he needs.

We know from our recent dialogue with a club insider that Koppen has begun looking for another striker, and Noble’s comments suggest those discussions are taking place alongside the coaching staff’s assessment of the squad.

Ultimately, I’m far more interested in whether Koppen signs the right players than whether he’s the first person through the door at Rush Green.

But if the people coaching the team and those recruiting for it are finally speaking openly about what West Ham need, that has to be encouraging.

Asian Markets Slide as Anthropic’s Debut Fails to Lift AI Stocks

📅 Nov 20, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

MSCI’s broadest Asia-Pacific index outside Japan fell 3.3% on November 20, 2026, leaving Asian stocks on track for their worst week since 2020 after Anthropic’s Nasdaq debut failed to bolster confidence in the wider AI trade, Reuters reported.

The move took the benchmark’s weekly loss to 8.5%, its worst weekly performance since March 2020, when COVID-19 was spreading globally, Reuters said.

Japan’s Nikkei lost 5%, bringing its weekly decline to 7.7%. South Korea’s KOSPI fell 5.2% and ended the week down 8%. Taiwan shares were down 6.2% on the day and 17.5% for the week, which Reuters said was their largest weekly decline since 1990.

The selling in Asia came after both the S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite lost more than 2% on November 19, 2026, despite a surge in Anthropic’s shares during their debut. Reuters described Anthropic as the world’s first publicly traded frontier AI company. Nasdaq’s historical data show that the Class A shares opened at $111.50, traded between $92.72 and $115.10, and closed at $97.17.

“Risk aversion intensified as investors continued to unwind crowded positioning and reassess the broader macro backdrop following Anthropic’s record-shattering IPO,” said Citi markets analyst Preston Pagli. He added that Nvidia’s earnings had not provided the reset markets wanted, and that sentiment deteriorated further when Anthropic’s debut failed to spark a broader rally.

Reuters identified three existing pressures on AI stocks: Jensen Huang’s departure from Nvidia, momentum behind the anti-data-center movement following Democrats’ sweep of the US midterm elections, and concerns about rising debt loads across the sector.

Goldman Sachs analysts said no single headline drove the retreat. Asset managers and hedge funds were net sellers of macro products while buying selected consumer and financial stocks, and poor liquidity exacerbated the price movements. That account points to broad repositioning as a key mechanism behind the steep index declines.

Euro Stoxx 50 futures were down 1.7% at 6,017 at the time of the report. S&P 500 futures fell 0.5% and Nasdaq futures declined 1% during Asian trading, indicating that selling pressure was extending beyond the region’s cash markets.

Reuters reported that the dollar index was steady at 103.86 and heading for a fourth consecutive weekly gain after reaching its highest level since April 2025 during the same week. The 10-year US Treasury yield fell for a fifth straight session to 5%, while the two-year yield touched a 10-day low of 4.7216% as investors moved into government bonds.

The contrast between Anthropic’s strong debut and the broader retreat separates demand for an individual listing from confidence in the sector as a whole. For private AI companies and their backers, that distinction is consequential: public investors embraced a single offering while reducing exposure to the wider sector, with data-center opposition and debt concerns still unresolved.

SoftBank suffers worst day in over two years as AI sell-off hits Asian tech stocks

📅 2026-11-20  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Shares in Japan's SoftBank Group closed 14.48% lower on Friday, marking their worst day since August 2024, as a broader sell-off swept through Asian technology stocks following the highly anticipated market debut of artificial intelligence startup Anthropic.

The sharp decline underscores growing investor concerns over SoftBank's aggressive bets on artificial intelligence, particularly its exposure to OpenAI, as markets question whether massive investments in AI infrastructure will generate sufficient returns.

The group is one of the biggest backers of OpenAI, Anthropic's rival, having invested a total of $64.6 billion in the ChatGPT maker.

SoftBank's heavy exposure to OpenAI as well as its ownership of chip designer Arm has made its shares particularly sensitive to shifts in sentiment toward the AI sector. The stock lost 30% in October and is down 31% so far this month.

Japanese semiconductor stocks also tumbled, with Tokyo Electron closing 7.06% lower, Lasertec shedding 9.21% and Renesas Electronics losing 7.02%.

The sell-off extended to South Korea, where Nvidia suppliers SK Hynix and Samsung Electronics fell 5.92% and 7.01%, respectively, pressuring the benchmark Kospi, which ended 5.21% lower.

Elsewhere in the region, Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Co, or TSMC, closed 4.76% lower in Taiwan.

As of the last hour of trading, Chinese tech giant Tencent was down 2.01%, while memory chipmaker CXMT fell 5.31% to a fresh record low, while Baidu dropped 4.79%. AI chipmaker Enflame, dubbed as one of China's "four little dragons" fell 7.16%.

The declines in Asia come after the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite fell more than 2% overnight following Anthropic's highly anticipated Wall Street debut.

"Gliene faccio un’altra io: perché non può vincerlo? Mi è venuto in mente il Leicester: non ci credeva nessuno, e invece ha vinto il titolo diventando la Cenerentola della Premier League. Io dico che la Lazio ha il 15% di portare a casa il campionato. Anche se la più forte resta sempre l’Inter".

Multi-Turn Agentic Reinforcement Learning with Tunix and Gemma 4 on Google TPUs

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In our previous posts - Easy FunctionGemma finetuning with Tunix on Google TPUs and Autonomous LLM post-training with Tunix on TPUs - we showed how Google Tunix streamlines Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and single-turn Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on Cloud TPUs. Real-world AI agents, however, rarely finish their job in a single turn. They have to reason over partial observations, invoke tools, observe environment feedback, and adapt their strategy across multiple steps. So in this post, we will walk through how to train a multi-turn, tool-using LLM agent using Tunix's agentic reinforcement learning pipeline on Cloud TPU v6e-8. You can find the complete code in this GitHub repository.

We will revisit the Plane Strike board game from an earlier TensorFlow/JAX reinforcement learning blog, replacing the custom MLP policy with Gemma 4 E2B in thinking mode on a single Cloud TPU v6e-8 host. Plane Strike is played on a 6x6 grid (A1 to F6) where one 8-cell plane is hidden in one of 4 orientations. Each turn, the agent sees the result of its previous strike and the raw visible board (. untried, X hit, O miss), thinks through candidate placements, and calls the strike(cell="...") tool to strike one cell. The game ends when all 8 plane cells are hit, or after 23 strikes.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Although google/gemma-4-E2B-it has decent spatial reasoning and tool calling capabilities, out of the box, it wins only 9.4% of games on held-out boards (averaging 22.44 strikes). After 100 steps of agentic GRPO with Tunix, its win rate reaches 95% while cutting the average game length to ~16 strikes.

We set up the multi-turn RL pipeline in four modular components:

Let's look at the key steps for each piece.

In env.py, we subclass Tunix's BaseTaskEnv to implement PlaneStrikeEnv. Each environment instance receives a task dictionary with a seed that fixes the hidden plane position so all 8 rollouts in a GRPO group play the exact same board:

On each turn, _step_impl evaluates the struck cell index against the hidden board, updates the visible grid, computes the step reward (summed into the trajectory-level reward over the episode), and returns EnvStepResult(observation, reward, done, info).

For standard multi-tool agents, tunix.rl.agentic also provides ready-to-use ToolEnvironment, ToolAgent and ToolManager abstractions out of the box so we don't have to write custom message/observation plumbing unless we need custom turn control.

In Gemma 4, tool schemas are declared in the system prompt inside <|tool>declaration:..., and a model turn that calls a tool ends with:

<|tool_response> is one of Gemma 4's native stop tokens: the model pauses there and waits for response:strike{value:<|"|>...<|"|>}, then continues generating its next thought and tool call within the same assistant turn.

In strike_tool.py, we define StrikeTool(BaseTool) and subclass Tunix's Gemma4ChatTemplateParser to append the tool declaration to the system prompt and render "tool" messages as in-turn responses:

In agent.py, PlaneStrikeAgent(ConversationAgentBase) translates between environment observations and model messages.

When the model finishes a turn, tool_strike ignores everything inside <|channel>thought... and parses the final answer after ; only a final answer with a single valid strike call counts as a valid board coordinate (otherwise INVALID_ACTION is returned). If the model stopped at <|tool_response>, the next board observation is appended with role="tool" so the model continues its turn:

In train_planestrike.py, we configure Tunix's asynchronous vllm rollout engine, RLEngine, and GRPOLearner:

Multi-turn agentic RL is often bottlenecked by variable-length rollout generation, so we set rollout_vllm_server_mode to true to enable continuous asynchronous multi-turn rollouts via vllm-tpu and enable_prefix_caching to true to true to cache the growing multi-turn conversation history. Since we are using a v6e-8 single host, the actor model, the reference model, and the rollout workers share the 8 chips.

We then pass our actor, tokenizer, ClusterConfig, GRPOConfig (loss_algo="gspo-token", advantage_estimator="rloo", exact_token_continuity=True to preserve exact token IDs across multi-turn rollouts without retokenization drift), agent, and environment into RLEngine and PlaneStrikeGRPOLearner:

All of these enable us to launch the 100-step training job with:

Over 100 steps (8 boards x 8 games per step), the agent learns both reliable tool calling and spatial deduction:

With Tunix's tunix.rl.agentic abstractions, vllm-tpu, and GRPOLearner, multi-turn, tool-using agentic RL on Cloud TPUs is easy and efficient. Check out the planestrike-gemma4-rl GitHub repository and read the Tunix documentation to train your own agentic model with Tunix and Google TPUs.

Automating the Full PR Lifecycle with Claude Task Master

📅 Nov 19, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

You open a pull request. CI runs. Something fails. You fix it, push again. A reviewer leaves comments. You address them, push again. CI passes. You wait for approval. You merge. Repeat for every PR.

This loop is mechanical. It doesn't require creativity - it requires persistence. And that's exactly what Claude Task Master handles.

The tool doesn't just retry - it understands the codebase, runs tests, and makes targeted fixes.

This repository's own PRs #147 through #150 were merged using claudetm merge-pr. The maintainer didn't babysit any of them.

merge-pr is one entry point. The full autonomous workflow starts with:

If you run multiple Claude subscriptions, profiles keep them isolated:

Each profile gets its own ~/.claudetm/profiles// directory so credentials never clobber each other.

The REST API, MCP server, and HMAC-signed webhooks expose the same lifecycle programmatically. A dashboard, a bot, or a CI pipeline can dispatch tasks and react to progress without running the CLI interactively.

Requires Python >= 3.10, authenticated Claude CLI, and authenticated GitHub CLI.
